\frac{\varepsilon}{2}N^{2}\Delta_{1}\psi=\frac{\varepsilon}{4F}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}

Collecting all the results we find:

\displaystyle S_{MN}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{R}{2}+3\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\varepsilon}{4F}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)}{4F^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{R}{2}+3\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{4F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\varepsilon}{4F}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)}{4F^{2}}\right]

or

S_{MN}=\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{R}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(\frac{3\varepsilon F_{\psi}^{2}-F}{2F^{2}}\right)\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)}{4F^{2}}\right].

(9.7)

We introduce the scalar field
\Psi
with the requirement:

d\Psi=\sqrt{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon F_{\psi}^{2}-F}{2F^{2}}\right)}d\psi

(9.8)

and the action becomes

S_{MN}=\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{R}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Psi_{;i}\Psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)}{4F\left(\Psi\right)^{2}}\right].

(9.9)

We conclude that the scalar field
\Psi
is minimally coupled to the
gravitational field. Therefore we have proved that to every non minimally
coupled scalar field we may associate a unique minimally coupled scalar
field in a conformally related space and an appropriate potential. Since all
considerations are reversible, the result is reversible.

In the following section we extend the above proposition to a general
V^{n}

Riemannian space.

9.3
Generalization to dimension
n

Consider the non minimally coupled scalar field
\psi
whose field
equations are obtained from the action:

\displaystyle S_{NM}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}N^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left[F\left(\psi\right)\bar{R}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}N^{-2}g^{ij}\psi_{,i}\psi_{,j}-\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}F\left(\psi\right)N^{n-2}R-2(n-1)F\left(\psi\right)N^{n-3}\Delta_{2}N+\\
-F\left(\psi\right)N^{n}(n-1)(n-4)\Delta_{1}N+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}N^{n-2}g^{ij}\psi_{,i}\psi_{,j}-N^{n}\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)\end{array}\right]

where we have substituted
[
150
]

\bar{R}=N^{-2}R-2(n-1)N^{-3}\Delta_{2}N-(n-1)(n-4)\Delta_{1}N

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{ij}N^{,i}N^{,j}

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{ij}N^{;ij}.

Define the function
N(x^{i})
in terms of the coupling function
F(\psi)

by the requirement:

N^{n-2}=\frac{1}{-2F}.~,~F=-\frac{N^{2-n}}{2}.

For each term of the action
S_{NM}
we have the following:

The first term gives:

\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(F\left(\psi\right)N^{n-2}R\right)=\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{R}{2}\right).

The second term gives:

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-2(n-1)F\left(\psi\right)N^{n-3}N_{;ij}g^{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left((n-1)N^{2-n}N^{n-3}N_{;ij}g^{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left((n-1)N^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\left(\sqrt{-g}g^{ij}N_{,k}\right)_{,j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left((n-1)N^{-1}N_{;ij}g^{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-(n-1)\left(N^{-1}\right)_{;j}N_{;i}g^{ij}\right).

Furthermore we compute

N=\frac{1}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{1}{n-2}}}\rightarrow N^{-1}=\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{1}{n-2}}

N_{;i}N_{;j}^{-1}=-\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}.

Replacing we find for the second term:

\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-2(n-1)F\left(\psi\right)N^{n-3}N_{;ij}g^{ij}\right)=\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}\right).

(note that this is the same with the previous expression for
n=4
).

The third term gives:

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(F\left(\psi\right)N^{n}(n-1)(n-4)\Delta_{1}N\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{N^{2-n}}{2}N^{n}(n-1)(n-4)\Delta_{1}N\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{1}{2}N^{2}(n-1)(n-4)\Delta_{1}N\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}\right).

Finally the the fourth term gives:

\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}N^{n-2}g^{ij}\psi_{,i}\psi_{,j}\right)=\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\frac{1}{F}g^{ij}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}\right).

Collecting the results for the last three terms we find

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\varepsilon\frac{1}{4F}g^{ij}\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}-\varepsilon\frac{1}{4F}\right)\psi_{;i}\psi_{;j}g^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(\frac{2\varepsilon(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}-\varepsilon\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}-\frac{1}{2F}\right)\psi_{,i}\psi_{,j}g^{ij}.

We define the new scalar field
\Psi
with the requirement

d\Psi=\left(\frac{2\varepsilon(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}-\varepsilon\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}-\frac{1}{2F}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d\psi.

In terms of
\Psi\
the action becomes

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(\frac{2\varepsilon(n-1)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{F^{2}}-\varepsilon\frac{(n-1)(n-4)}{\left(n-2\right)^{2}}\frac{F_{\psi}^{2}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{4}{2-n}}F^{2}}-\frac{1}{2F}\right)\psi_{,i}\psi_{,j}g^{ij}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Psi_{;i}\Psi_{;j}g^{ij}\right).

Collecting the above we find the action
S_{M}
of a minimally coupled
scalar field

S_{M}=\int dx^{n}\sqrt{-g}\left(-\frac{R}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Psi_{;i}\Psi_{;j}g^{ij}-\frac{\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{n}{n-2}}}\right).

We note that the new scalar field
\Psi
is minimally coupled to the
gravitational field
g_{ij}
and that the potential of
\Psi
is
\frac{\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{n}{n-2}}}
.

The above proof agrees with the one given in the paper of
[
151
]
.
However it is obviously simpler, more direct and clear.

9.4
Conformal Lagrangians in scalar field cosmology

In this section we apply the conformal transformation in the Lagrangian of
the field equations in a FRW spatially flat spacetime.

We consider the flat FRW
\left(K=0\right)
spacetime whose metric is

ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+a^{2}\left(t\right)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}

(9.11)

where
\delta_{ij}
is the 3-space metric in Cartesian coordinates. The
Lagrangian of a scalar field
\phi
minimally coupled to gravity in these
coordinates is

L_{M}\left(a,\dot{a},\phi,\dot{\phi}\right)=-3a\dot{a}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}a^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}-a^{3}V\left(\phi\right).

(9.12)

The Lagrangian for a non minimally coupled scalar field
\psi
is

L_{NM}\left(a,\dot{a},\psi,\dot{\psi}\right)=6F\left(\psi\right)a\dot{a}^{2}+6F_{\psi}\left(\psi\right)a^{2}\dot{a}\dot{\psi}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}a^{3}\dot{\psi}^{2}-a^{3}V\left(\psi\right)

(9.13)

where
\ F(\psi)<0
is the coupling function and
\varepsilon=\pm 1
where

\varepsilon=1
for real scalar field and
\varepsilon=-1
for phantom
field. The Hamiltonian for the Lagrangian (
9.13
) is

E=6F\left(\psi\right)a\dot{a}^{2}+6F_{\psi}\left(\psi\right)a^{2}\dot{a}\dot{\psi}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}a^{3}\dot{\psi}^{2}+a^{3}V\left(\psi\right).

(9.14)

We construct a conformal Lagrangian which corresponds to a minimally coupled
scalar field.

To do that we consider first a change in the scale factor from
a(t)

\rightarrow A(t)
defined by the formula (see
[
152
,
140
]
)

A\left(t\right)=\sqrt{-2F}a\left(t\right)\text{ }

(9.15)

Then the Lagrangian (
9.13
) takes the form:

L_{NM}\left(A,\dot{A},\psi,\dot{\psi}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(\frac{3\varepsilon F_{\psi}^{2}-F}{2F^{2}}\right)A^{3}~\dot{\psi}^{2}\right]-\frac{A^{3}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}V\left(\psi\right)

(9.16)

that is, the cross term
\dot{a}\dot{\psi}
disappears.

Next we consider the coordinate transformation:

d\Psi=\sqrt{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon F_{\psi}^{2}-F}{2F^{2}}\right)}~d\psi~

(9.17)

and Lagrangian (
9.16
) becomes:

L_{NM}\left(A,A^{\prime},\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}A^{3}~\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]~,~\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)=\frac{V\left(\Psi\right)}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}

(9.18)

The form of the Lagrangian (
9.18
) is (
9.12
) hence the
previous result applies and under the conformal transformation

d\tau=\sqrt{-2F\left(\psi\right)}dt

(9.19)

the Lagrangian (
9.18
) becomes:

L_{M}\left(A,\dot{A},\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\right)=-3AA^{\prime 2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}A^{3}\Psi^{\prime 2}-\frac{A^{3}}{\left(-2F\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}V\left(\Psi\right)

(9.20)

where a prime
′
indicates derivative wrt the new coordinate
\tau.

We note that if in the new coordinates
\tau,x^{i}
we consider the metric

d\bar{s}^{2}=-d\tau^{2}+A^{2}\left(\tau\right)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}

(9.21)

then the term
3AA^{\prime 2}
equals the Ricci scalar
\bar{R}
of the
conformally flat metric
d\bar{s}^{2}.
Therefore the Lagrangian (
9.20
) can be seen as the Lagrangian of a scalar field
\Psi
of
potential
\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)
minimally coupled to the
gravitational filed
\bar{g}_{ij}
in the space with metric
d\bar{s}^{2}.

Replacing the coordinate
\tau
and the quantity
A\left(\tau\right)

from (
9.15
), (
9.19
), we find:

d\bar{s}^{2}=\sqrt{-2F}(-dt^{2}+a^{2}\left(t\right)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j})=\sqrt{-2F}ds^{2}

(9.22)

that is, the metric
d\bar{s}^{2}
is conformally related to the metric
ds^{2}
with conformal function
\sqrt{-2F}.
This means that the
non-minimally coupled scalar field in the gravitational field
ds^{2}
is
equivalent to a minimally coupled scalar field - with appropriate potential
defined in terms of the conformal function - in the gravitational field
d\bar{s},
the resutl is reversible. Equivalently the Lagrangians
L_{M},

L_{NM}
are conformally related and the field equations (the Euler-Lagrange
equations) are invariant under the conformal transformation if the
Hamiltonian constrains
H_{M},~H_{NM}
vanish (see Lemma
7.2.2
).

In the following sections we apply the Noether symmetry approach as a
geometric selection rule in order to determine the dark energy models; that
is, we search for dark energy models by requiring the field equations to
admit Noether point symmetries.

9.5
Noether point symmetries of a non minimally coupled Scalar field.

Consider a non-minimally coupled scalar field with action

S_{NM}=\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[F\left(\psi\right)R+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\bar{g}_{ij}\psi^{;i}\psi^{;j}-\bar{V}\left(\psi\right)\right]+\int L_{m}d\tau dx^{3}

in a flat FRW space-time, whose metric in Cartesian coordinates is

ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+a^{2}\left(t\right)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}

and
L_{m}
is the Lagrangian of dust matter of density
\rho_{D}
(for
commoving observers).

The Lagrangian of the field equations is (
9.13
) are the Hamiltonian
(total energy density) (
9.14
) and the Euler-Lagrange equations

\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L_{NM}}{\partial\left(\dot{a},\dot{\psi}\right)}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\left(a,\psi\right)}=0.

If the Hamiltonian (
9.14
) is
E\neq 0
then the space admits dust
which, however does not interact with the scalar field and has energy
density
\rho_{D}=\frac{\left|E\right|}{a^{3}}.
If
E\,

=0
the space does not admit dust. In the following we determine
all potentials
V\left(\psi\right)
for which this dynamical system
admits Noether point symmetries beyond the trivial one
\partial_{t}
.
Subsequently we use the resulting Noether integrals to find analytical
solutions for the field equations for each of these potentials.

In order to determine the Noether point symmetries of the Lagrangian (
9.13
) we shall follow the results of chapter
4
. That is we
brake the Lagrangian in the kinematic part which defines the kinematic
metric and the remaining part which we consider to be the potential. Then we
apply theorem
4.3.2
which
states that the Noether point symmetries of the Lagrangian follow form the
homothetic algebra of the kinematic metric. The kinematic metric admits a
non-trivial homothetic (not necessarily proper homothetic) algebra if a
given condition is satisfied which involves the symmetry vector and the
potential. The solution of this relation provides all the potentials for
which extra Noether symmetries are admitted. We use the Noether integrals of
these extra Noether symmetries to find an analytic solution for each of the
corresponding potentials..

From the Lagrangian (
9.13
) we define the kinematic metric:

ds_{KM}^{2}=12F\left(\psi\right)a\dot{a}^{2}+12F_{\psi}\left(\psi\right)a^{2}\dot{a}\dot{\psi}+\varepsilon a^{3}\dot{\psi}^{2}.

(9.23)

This is a two dimensional metric in the space
(a,\psi).
Because the
homothetic algebra of a 2 dimensional metric is different for a flat and a
non-flat (but conformally flat because all two dimensional spaces are
conformally flat) we consider the case the metric (
9.23
) is
maximally symmetric
2
2

2

The Ricci scalar is
R=K~
where
K
is a constant
.

The Ricci scalar of the metric (
9.23
) is computed to be:

R_{\left(KM\right)}=\frac{\varepsilon}{4a^{3}}\frac{\left(2F_{\psi\psi}F-F_{\psi}^{2}\right)}{\left(\varepsilon F-3F_{\psi}^{2}\right)^{2}}

(9.24)

Hence if the metric (
9.23
) is maximally symmetric then it follows
that
R_{\left(KM\right)}=0
, that is, the kinetic metric (
9.23
)
must be flat.

9.5.1
Case A.
R_{KM}=0

Condition
R_{KM}=0
and (
9.24
) give:

2F_{\psi\psi}F-F_{\psi}^{2}=0

(9.25)

provided

\varepsilon F-3F_{\psi}^{2}\neq 0.

(9.26)

The solution of (
9.25
) is

F\left(\psi\right)=-\frac{F_{0}\varepsilon}{12}\left(\psi+\psi_{0}\right)^{2}

(9.27)

where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
and
F_{0}\varepsilon>0
. We note that this
F\left(\psi\right)
satisfies condition (
9.26
) therefore it is
acceptable.

In order to determine the homothetic algebra of the kinematic metric (
9.23
) we write it in a more familiar form. We introduce the coordinates
A,\Psi
by the relations

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{-2F}a

(9.28)

\displaystyle d\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon F_{\psi}^{2}-F}{2F^{2}}\right)}~d\psi.

(9.29)

In the new coordinates
A,\Psi
the metric (
9.23
), the Lagrangian (
9.13
) and the non minimal coupling function
F\left(\Psi\right)

take the following form

For
\varepsilon=1
and
F_{0}>0~

\displaystyle ds_{KM}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]

(9.30)

\displaystyle L_{M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]-\frac{A^{3}}{\left(-2F\left(\Psi\right)\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}V\left(\Psi\right)~.

(9.31)

\displaystyle F\left(\Psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{F_{0}}{12}\exp\left(\pm\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{F_{0}}{F_{0}+1}}\Psi\right)~,~F_{0}>0

(9.32)

For
\varepsilon=-1~\,
and
-1<F_{0}<0~

\displaystyle ds_{KM}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]

(9.33)

\displaystyle L_{NM}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]-\frac{A^{3}}{\left(-2F\left(\Psi\right)\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}V\left(\Psi\right)

(9.34)

\displaystyle F\left(\Psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\left|F_{0}\right|}{12}\exp\left(\pm\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\left|F_{0}\right|}{1-\left|F_{0}\right|}}\Psi\right)~,~-1<F_{0}<0

(9.35)

For
\varepsilon=-1~
and
F_{0}<-1
we introduce a new real field
\Psi^{\ast}=i\Psi
and get the real field
F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)

\displaystyle ds_{KM}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{\ast 2}\right]

(9.36)

\displaystyle L_{NM}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)}}\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{\ast 2}\right]-\frac{A^{3}}{\left(-2F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}V\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)

(9.37)

\displaystyle F\left(\Psi^{\ast}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\left|F_{0}\right|}{12}\exp\left(\mp\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\left|F_{0}\right|}{\left|F_{0}\right|-1}}~\Psi^{\ast}\right)~,~F_{0}<-1.

(9.38)

We see that in the region
-\infty<F_{0}<-1
starting from a phantom field
\Psi
we end up with a real scalar field
\Psi^{\ast}.

In order to consider the three Lagrangians (
9.31
),(
9.34
),(
9.37
) at the same time we consider the Lagrangian

L=N^{2}\left(\Psi\right)\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{k}}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right]-A^{3}\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)

(9.39)

where
N^{2}\left(\Psi\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\Psi\right)}}~
and
\bar{V}\left(\Psi\right)=\frac{V\left(\Psi\right)}{\left(-2F\left(\Psi\right)\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}
where
F\left(\Psi\right)<0
. The constant
\varepsilon_{k}=\pm 1
where the value
+1
is for
\varepsilon=1,~F_{0}>0~
and
\varepsilon=-1~,~F_{0}<-1
and the value
-1
is for
\varepsilon=-1~,~-1<F_{0}<0.
Then the new kinematic metric
is written as

ds_{KM}^{2}=N^{2}\left(\Psi\right)\left[-3A\dot{A}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{k}}{2}A^{3}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\right].

(9.40)

We simplify this metric by introducing new coordinates
r,\theta
defined
by the transformation:

r=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}A^{\frac{3}{2}}~,~\theta=\sqrt{\frac{3\varepsilon_{k}}{8}}~\Psi

(9.41)

This step is necessary in order to deduce the homothetic algebra of the
metric from well known previous results. In the new coordinates the metric (
9.40
) takes the simple form:

ds_{KM}^{2}=N^{2}\left(\theta\right)\left(-dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2}\right).

(9.42)

that is, it is directly related to the flat 2d Lorentzian space with metric

ds^{2}=-dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2}

with conformal factor
N^{2}\left(\theta\right).
In the new coordinates
the curvature scalar is

R_{KM}=-\frac{2}{r^{4}N^{3}\left(\theta\right)}\left(N_{,\theta\theta}-\frac{1}{N\left(\theta\right)}\left(N_{,\theta}\right)^{2}\right)

(9.43)

hence the condition
R_{KM}=0
gives the function
N\left(\theta\right)
:

N\left(\theta\right)=N_{0}e^{k\theta}~,~k\in\mathbb{C}~,N_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

(9.44)

where
k
is a new constant.

Taking this into account we have that in the
r,\theta
coordinates the
Lagrangian (
9.39
) becomes:

L=N_{0}^{2}e^{2k\theta}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}\right)-r^{2}\bar{V}\left(\theta\right).

(9.45)

The constant
k
is related to the previous constant
F_{0}
via the
function
N(\theta)
. Using (
9.41
) we express
N\left(\theta\right)
in terms of
\Psi
:

N\left(\Psi\right)=N_{0}e^{k\sqrt{\frac{3\varepsilon_{k}}{8}}\Psi}.

(9.46)

Comparing with
F\left(\Psi\right)
and eliminating
N\left(\Psi\right)
we find:

F\left(\Psi\right)=-\frac{1}{2N_{0}^{4}}e^{-4k~\sqrt{\frac{3\varepsilon}{8}}~\Psi}.

(9.47)

This is a second expression of
F\left(\Psi\right)
in terms of the
constant
k.
Comparing with the previous expression (
9.32
),(
9.35
) and (
9.38
), which expresses

in

F_{0}
(and holds for all ranges of values of

we find:

-\frac{F_{0}\varepsilon}{12}\exp\left(\pm\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon F_{0}}{F_{0}+1}}\Psi\right)=-\frac{1}{2N_{0}^{4}}e^{-4k~\sqrt{\frac{3\varepsilon_{k}}{8}}~\Psi}.

(9.48)

This relation must hold identically which leads to the conditions

~N_{0}=\left(\frac{6}{\varepsilon F_{0}}\right)^{1/4}

(9.49)

and

~\left|k\right|=\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon F_{0}}{F_{0}+1}}.

(9.50)

In Appendix
9.A
we give the relation between the various
ranges of the constant
F_{0}
and the corresponding ranges of the constant

\left|k\right|.

Case
\left|k\right|\neq 1
.

For

\displaystyle K^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\left(1-k\right)\theta}r^{k}}{N_{0}^{2}}\left(-\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle K^{2}

\displaystyle=

the non gradient KV

For this potential the Noether symmetries form the
sl\left(2,R\right)

Lie algebra, i.e the dynamical system is the two dimensional Kepler-Ermakov
system Therefore it admits the Ermakov - Pinney invariant which we may
construct with the use of the Noether symmetries or with the use of the
corresponding Killing Tensor (see Proposition
5.6.2
)

5.

The case
V\left(\theta\right)=0
corresponds the free particle
(see chapter
2
).

Case
\left|k\right|=1

We have to consider two cases i.e.
k=1
and
k=-1.

Case
k=1

The KVs of the kinematic metric are the
K_{k=1}^{1,2}
of (
9.51
,
9.52
) and the vector

K_{k=1}^{3}=-r\left(\ln\left(re^{-\theta}\right)-1\right)\partial_{r}+\ln\left(re^{-\theta}\right)\partial_{\theta}.

(9.62)

The vectors
K_{k=1}^{1,2}
are gradient and
K^{3}
is non-gradient. The
HV is gradient and it is given by

H^{i}=\frac{1}{4}r\left(2\ln\left(re^{-\theta}\right)+3\right)\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln\left(re^{-\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\right)\partial_{\theta}

(9.63)

Case
k=-1

The KVs of the kinematic metric are
K_{k=-1}^{1,2}
of (
9.51
,
9.52
) and the vector

\bar{K}^{3}=r\left(\ln\left(re^{\theta}\right)-1\right)\partial_{r}+\ln\left(re^{\theta}\right)\partial_{\theta}.

(9.64)

The vectors
\bar{K}^{1,2}
are gradient and
\bar{K}^{3}
is non-gradient.
The gradient HV is given by

\bar{H}^{i}=\frac{1}{4}r\left(2\ln\left(re^{\theta}\right)+3\right)\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln\left(re^{\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\right)\partial_{\theta}

(9.65)

In the following we consider only the case
k=1
. The results for the case
k=-1
are found if we make the change
\theta_{\left(k=-1\right)}=-\bar{\theta}
.

Using theorem
4.3.2
and
making simple calculations we find the following results

1.

Noether symmetries generated by the KV
K^{1}
.

a) If
V\left(\theta\right)=V_{0}e^{2\theta}~
then we have the extra
Noether symmetries
K^{1}~,~tK^{1}
with Noether integrals the (
9.55
) with
k=1
.

b) If
V\left(\theta\right)=\left(V_{0}e^{2\theta}-\frac{m}{2}\theta e^{2\theta}\right)
, then we have the Noether symmetries
e^{\pm\sqrt{m}t}K^{1}
with Noether integrals (
9.56
) with
k=1
.

2.

Noether symmetries generated by the KV
K^{2}
.

a) If
V\left(\theta\right)=V_{0}e^{-2\theta}
then we have the Noether
symmetries
K^{2}~,~tK^{2}
with Noether integrals

I_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\theta-\ln r\right)~,~I_{2}^{\prime}=t\left[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\theta-\ln r\right)\right]-\left(\theta-\ln r\right)

(9.66)

b) If
V\left(\theta\right)=V_{0}e^{-2\theta}-\frac{1}{4}pe^{2\theta}\,

then we have the Noether symmetries
K^{2}~,~tK^{2}
with Noether integrals

I_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\theta-\ln r\right)-pt~,~I_{2}^{\prime}=t\left[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\theta-\ln r\right)\right]-\left(\theta-\ln r\right)-\frac{1}{2}pt^{2}

(9.67)

3.

If
V\left(\theta\right)=0
then the system becomes the free
particle and admits seven extra Noether symmetries.

As we have remarked the results for
k=-1
are obtained directly from those
for
k=1
if we make the substitution
\theta_{\left(k=-1\right)}=-\bar{\theta}
. Therefore there is no need to state them explicitly.

In the next section using the Noether symmetries for the potentials we have
found, we determine the analytic solution for each case.

9.5.2
Case A: Analytic solutions for
k=1

We introduce new coordinates
u,v
by the relations

u=\left(\theta-\ln r\right)~,~v=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\theta}r^{2}

(9.68)

with inverse relations:

r=\left(2ve^{-2u}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}~,~~\theta=\frac{1}{4}\ln\left(2ve^{2u}\right).

(9.69)

In the new coordinates
u,v
the Lagrangian (
9.45
) of the field
equations becomes

L\left(u,v,\dot{u},\dot{v}\right)=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(\dot{u}\dot{v}\right)-U\left(u,v\right)

(9.70)

and the field equations are

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(\dot{u}\dot{v}\right)+U\left(u,v\right)

\displaystyle\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\ddot{u}+U_{,v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\ddot{v}+U_{,u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

where the potential
U\left(u,v\right)
is one of the potentials we have
found in the last section. In the new coordinates we have (
p,m
are
constants; recall that in general
U(r,\theta)=r^{2}V\left(\theta\right)
):

•

U(r,\theta)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\theta}\rightarrow U\left(u,v\right)=V_{0}e^{-2u}

(9.71)

•

U(r,\theta)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{2\theta}\rightarrow U\left(u,v\right)=2V_{0}v

(9.72)

•

U(r,\theta)=r^{2}\left(V_{0}e^{-2\theta}-\frac{1}{4}pe^{2\theta}\right)\rightarrow U\left(u,v\right)=V_{0}e^{-2u}-\frac{p}{2}v

(9.73)

•

U(r,\theta)=r^{2}\left(V_{0}e^{2\theta}-\frac{m}{2}\theta e^{2\theta}\right)\rightarrow U\left(u,v\right)=2\left(V_{0}-m\frac{\ln 2}{8}\right)v-\frac{m}{4}v\ln v-\frac{m}{2}uv

(9.74)

•

U(r,\theta)=0\rightarrow U\left(u,v\right)=0

(9.75)

The Hamiltonian equals

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(\dot{u}\dot{v}\right)+U\left(u,v\right).

(9.76)

We write Lagrange equations for each potential and solve them taking into
consideration the first integrals for each Noether symmetry we have found
and the constraint imposed by the Hamiltonian. For each of the potentials
above we find the corresponding analytic solution given below.

•

U\left(u,v\right)=V_{0}e^{-2u}

u\left(t\right)=u_{1}t+u_{2},\text{ }v\left(t\right)=\frac{e^{-2u_{2}}}{u_{1}^{2}}\frac{V_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}e^{-2u_{1}t}+v_{1}t+v_{2}

(9.77)

with Hamiltonian constraint

E=\frac{u_{1}v_{1}}{2N_{0}^{2}}.

(9.78)

•

U\left(u,v\right)=2V_{0}v

u\left(t\right)=-\frac{2V_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}t^{2}+u_{1}t+u_{2},v\left(t\right)=v_{1}t+v_{2}

(9.79)

with Hamiltonian constrain

E=2V_{0}v_{2}+N_{0}^{2}\frac{u_{1}v_{1}}{2}.

(9.80)

•

U\left(u,v\right)=V_{0}e^{-2u}-\frac{p}{2}v

where

v_{1}=\frac{2V_{0}\sqrt{\pi}}{p^{\frac{3}{2}}N_{0}}\exp\left(\frac{u_{1}^{2}N_{0}^{2}}{p}-2u_{2}\right)~,~v_{2}=\frac{2V_{0}}{p}e^{-2u_{2}}

(9.81)

with Hamiltonian constraint

E=N_{0}^{2}\frac{v_{3}u_{1}}{2}-\frac{v_{4}p}{2}.

(9.82)

•

U\left(u,v\right)=2\left(V_{0}-m\frac{\ln 2}{8}\right)v-\frac{m}{4}v\ln v-\frac{m}{2}uv

\displaystyle u\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{1}e^{\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}+u_{2}e^{-\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}-\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2N_{0}}t+\frac{4V_{0}}{m}-\ln\left(\sqrt{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}~,~

(9.83)

\displaystyle v\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}~~,~E=-u_{2}m

(9.84)

\displaystyle u\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{1}e^{\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}+u_{2}e^{-\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}+\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2N_{0}}t+\frac{4V_{0}}{m}-\ln\left(\sqrt{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}~,~

(9.85)

\displaystyle v\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{N_{0}}\sqrt{m}t}~,~E=-u_{1}m.

(9.86)

•

U\left(u,v\right)=0
(the free particle)

u\left(t\right)=u_{1}t+u_{2}~,~v\left(t\right)=v_{1}t+v_{2}

(9.87)

with Hamiltonian constraint

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}u_{1}v_{1}.

(9.88)

9.5.3
Case A: Analytic solutions for
\left|k\right|\neq 1

When
\left|k\right|\neq 1
we have to consider two cases
k^{2}>1
and
k^{2}<1.
Both cases it is convenient to be discussed if we
use as variables the functions
S_{1}\left(r,\theta\right),

S_{2}\left(r,\theta\right)
which generate the Killing vectors (i.e.
K^{I,i}=S_{I}^{,i}

I=1,2).
A standard calculation gives (see appendix B
for details) :

\displaystyle K^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\left(1-k\right)\theta}r^{k}}{N_{0}^{2}}\left(-\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\right)~,~S_{1}\left(r,\theta\right)=\frac{r^{1+k}e^{\left(1+k\right)\theta}}{\left(k+1\right)}

(9.89)

\displaystyle K^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\left(1+k\right)\theta}r^{-k}}{N_{0}^{2}}\left(\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\right),~S_{2}\left(r,\theta\right)=\frac{r^{1-k}e^{-\left(1-k\right)\theta}}{k-1}.

(9.90)

Because the metric is flat the new variables
S_{1}\left(r,\theta\right),

S_{2}\left(r,\theta\right)
are Cartesian and will be denoted with
x,y.

So we write:

x=\frac{r^{1+k}e^{-\left(1+k\right)\theta}}{\left(k+1\right)}~,~y=\frac{r^{1-k}e^{\left(-1+k\right)\theta}}{k-1}.

(9.91)

The inverse transformation is:

for
k^{2}>1

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\left(1-k^{2}\right)}\ln\left(\frac{\left(k^{2}-1\right)^{1-k}}{\left(k-1\right)^{2}}\frac{x^{1-k}}{y^{1+k}}\right)

(9.92)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left(k^{2}-1\right)xy}\left(\frac{\left(k^{2}-1\right)^{1-k}}{\left(k-1\right)^{2}}\frac{x^{1-k}}{y^{1+k}}\right)^{\frac{k}{2\left(k^{2}-1\right)}}

(9.93)

and

for
k^{2}<1

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\left(1-k^{2}\right)}\ln\left(\frac{\left(1-k^{2}\right)^{1-k}}{\left(1-k\right)^{2}}\frac{\bar{x}^{1-k}}{\bar{y}^{1+k}}\right)

(9.94)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}\right)\bar{x}\bar{y}}\left(\frac{\left(1-k^{2}\right)^{1-k}}{\left(1-k\right)^{2}}\frac{\bar{x}^{1-k}}{\bar{y}^{1+k}}\right)^{\frac{k}{2\left(k^{2}-1\right)}}

(9.95)

Note that in the second case we have written
\bar{x},\bar{y}
while we have
kept the
x,y
notation for the case
k^{2}>1.

The case
k^{2}>1

Before we look for analytic solutions we transform the Lagrangian in
the canonical coordinates
x,y.
Using the transformation relations (
9.92
), (
9.93
) we find that in the coordinates
x,y
the
Lagrangian (
9.43
) takes the form

L\left(x,y,\dot{x},\dot{y}\right)=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\dot{x}\dot{y}-U\left(x,y\right)

(9.96)

where
U\left(x,y\right)=r^{2}V\left(\theta\right)
where
V\left(\theta\right)
is one of the potentials computed above. In the coordinates

x,y
these potentials are as follows:

•

U_{1}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{2k\theta}=V_{0}\left(k^{2}-1\right)xy

(9.97)

U_{2}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{+2\theta}=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~x^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}

(9.98)

U_{3}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\theta}=V_{0}\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}y^{\frac{2}{1-k}}

(9.99)

•

U_{4}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\frac{\left(k^{2}-2\right)}{k}\theta}=V_{0}\frac{\left(k^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}}{\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{4}{k}}}\frac{1}{y^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}.

To the potentials
U_{2},U_{3},

U_{4}
we have to add three more which we
obtain by the addition of the potential of the harmonic oscillator.
Therefore finally we have 7 potentials. The extra potentials are

•

\displaystyle U_{5}\left(x,y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{0}r^{2}e^{+2\theta}+mr^{2}e^{2k\theta}=\bar{V}_{0+}~x^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}+\bar{m}xy

(9.100)

\displaystyle U_{6}\left(x,y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2}e^{-2\theta}+mr^{2}e^{2k\theta}=\bar{V}_{0-}y^{\frac{2}{1-k}}+\bar{m}xy

(9.101)

where
\bar{V}_{0+}=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~,~\bar{V}_{0-}=\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}~,~\bar{m}=m\left(k^{2}-1\right)

•

U_{7}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\frac{\left(k^{2}-2\right)}{k}\theta}+mr^{2}e^{2k\theta}=\bar{V}_{0}\frac{1}{y^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}+\bar{m}xy

(9.102)

where
\bar{V}_{0}=V_{0}\frac{\left(k^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}}{\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{4}{k}}}
.

•

And the free particle potential

U_{8}\left(x,y\right)=0

(9.103)

These expressions allow us to write for each potential the Lagrangian and
the Hamiltonian constraint in the coordinates
x,y
. This means that we
obtain the corresponding field equations in the coordinates
x,y
to
determine their solution.

Analytic solutions

The solution of the field equations for each potential is a formal and
lengthy operation which adds nothing but unnecessary material to the matter.
What is interesting is of course the final answer for each case and this is
what we give below for each of the potentials above.

•

U_{1}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}\left(k^{2}-1\right)xy

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}\sin\left(\omega t\right)+x_{2}\cos\left(\omega t\right)

(9.104)

\displaystyle y\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{1}\sin\left(\omega t\right)+y_{2}\cos\left(\omega t\right)

(9.105)

where
\omega^{2}=\frac{2V_{0}\left(k^{2}-1\right)}{N_{0}^{2}}
and the
Hamiltonian is

E=V_{0}\left(k^{2}-1\right)\left(x_{1}y_{1}+x_{2}y_{2}\right)

•

U_{2}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~x^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}

When
k\neq-3

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}t+x_{2}

(9.106)

\displaystyle y\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\bar{V}\left(k+1\right)\left(x_{1}t+x_{2}\right)^{\left(1+\frac{2}{1+k}\right)}}{x_{1}^{2}\left(3+k\right)N_{0}^{2}}+y_{1}t+y_{2}

(9.107)

where
\bar{V}=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}},
and the Hamiltonian
is

E=\frac{y_{1}x_{1}N_{0}^{2}}{2}.

When
k=-3

y\left(t\right)=-2\frac{\bar{V}}{N_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}}\ln\left(x_{1}t+x_{2}\right)+y_{1}t+y_{2}.

(9.108)

x(t),

E
being the same.

•

U_{3}\left(x,y\right)=V_{0}\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}y^{\frac{2}{1-k}}

When
k\neq 3

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\bar{V}\left(k-1\right)\left(y_{1}t+y_{2}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{k-1}}}{y_{1}^{2}\left(k-3\right)N_{0}^{2}}+x_{1}t+x_{2}

(9.109)

\displaystyle y\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{1}t+y_{2}

(9.110)

where
\bar{V}=V_{0}\left(k-1\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}
,
k\neq 3
and the
Hamiltonian is

E=\frac{y_{1}x_{1}N_{0}^{2}}{2}.

When
k=3~;

x\left(t\right)=-\frac{2\bar{V}}{N_{0}^{2}y_{1}^{2}}\ln\left(y_{1}t+y_{2}\right)+x_{1}t+x_{2}.

(9.111)

y(t),E
being the same

•

U_{5}\left(x,y\right)=\bar{V}_{0+}~x^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}+\bar{m}xy

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}\sin\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)

(9.112)

\displaystyle y\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)\left(y_{1}+2\frac{\omega}{\bar{m}}\int\frac{y_{2}-x_{1}\bar{V}_{0+}\sin\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}}{x_{1}\left(\cos\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)+1\right)}dt\right)

(9.113)

where
\omega^{2}=\frac{2\bar{m}}{N_{0}^{2}}
and
E=y_{2}.

•

U_{6}\left(x,y\right)=r^{2}e^{-2\theta}+mr^{2}e^{2k\theta}=\bar{V}_{0-}y^{\frac{2}{1-k}}+\bar{m}xy

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)\left(x_{1}+2\frac{\omega}{\bar{m}}\int\frac{x_{2}-y_{1}\bar{V}_{0-}\sin\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}}{y_{1}\left(\cos\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)+1\right)}dt\right)

(9.114)

\displaystyle y\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{1}\sin\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)

(9.115)

where
\omega^{2}=\frac{2\bar{m}}{N_{0}^{2}}
and
E=x_{2}.

•

U_{4,7}\left(x,y\right)=\bar{V}_{0}\frac{1}{y^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}+\bar{m}xy
.
\left(U_{4}\text{ is for }\bar{m}=0\right)

This is the Ermakov Pinney system. To solve it, it is convenient to go to
spherical coordinates. We consider the coordinate transformation

x=ze^{w}~,~y=ze^{-w}~

(9.116)

and the Lagrangian takes the form

L\left(z,w,\dot{z},\dot{w}\right)=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(\dot{z}^{2}-z^{2}\dot{w}^{2}\right)-\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{z^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}-\bar{m}z^{2}

(9.117)

whereas the Hamiltonian becomes

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(\dot{z}^{2}-z^{2}\dot{w}^{2}\right)+\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{z^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}+\bar{m}z^{2}.

(9.118)

This system admits the Ermakov-Lewis invariant, which is

J_{EL}=z^{4}\dot{w}^{2}-2\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}.

(9.119)

Using the Ermakov invariant the Hamiltonian becomes

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\dot{z}^{2}-N_{0}^{2}\frac{J_{EL}}{2z^{2}}+\bar{m}z^{2}.

(9.120)

This is the Hamiltonian of the Ermakov Pinney equation:

\ddot{z}+2\bar{m}z+N_{0}^{2}\frac{J_{EL}}{z^{3}}=0

(9.121)

whose solution is

\displaystyle z\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(l_{0}z_{1}\left(t\right)+l_{1}z_{2}\left(t\right)+l_{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(9.122)

\displaystyle e^{\frac{4}{k}w\left(t\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N_{0}^{2}J_{EL}}{2\bar{V}_{0}}\left[\tanh^{2}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{J_{EL}}}{k}\left(\int\frac{dt}{z^{2}\left(t\right)}+l_{4}\right)\right)-1\right]

(9.123)

where
z_{1,2}\left(t\right)
are solutions of the differential equation
\ddot{z}+2\bar{m}z=0
and
l_{0-4}
are constants.

•

U_{8}(x,y)=0

This is the free particle whose solution is

\displaystyle x\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}t+x_{2}~,~y\left(t\right)=y_{1}t+y_{2}

(9.124)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}x_{1}y_{1}

(9.125)

The case
k^{2}<1

In this case the canonical coordinates are the
\bar{x},\bar{y}.
Using the
transformation equations (
9.94
), (
9.95
) we write the
Lagrangian (
9.43
) as follows:

L\left(\bar{x},\bar{y},\overset{\cdot}{\bar{x},}\overset{\cdot}{\bar{y}}\right)=-\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\overset{\cdot}{\bar{x}}\overset{\cdot}{\bar{y}}-\bar{U}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)

(9.126)

where
U\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=r^{2}V\left(\theta\right)
the
potentials
V\left(\theta\right)
being as above. In the coordinates
\bar{x},\bar{y}
the potentials
U\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)
are:

•

\bar{U}_{1}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{2k\theta}=V_{0}\left(1-k^{2}\right)\bar{x}\bar{y}

(9.127)

\bar{U}_{2}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{+2\theta}=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~\bar{x}^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}

(9.128)

\bar{U}_{3}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\theta}=V_{0}\left(1-k\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}\bar{y}^{\frac{2}{1-k}}

(9.129)

•

\bar{U}_{4}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\frac{\left(k^{2}-2\right)}{k}\theta}=V_{0}\frac{\left(1-k^{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}}{\left(1-k\right)^{\frac{4}{k}}}\frac{1}{\bar{y}^{2}}\left(\frac{\bar{x}}{\bar{y}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}

(9.130)

As before we have three more potentials

•

\displaystyle\bar{U}_{5}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{0}r^{2}e^{+2\theta}=\bar{V}_{0+}~\bar{x}^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}+\bar{m}\bar{x}\bar{y}

(9.131)

\displaystyle\bar{U}_{6}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\theta}=\bar{V}_{0-}\bar{y}^{\frac{2}{1-k}}+\bar{m}\bar{x}\bar{y}

(9.132)

where
\bar{V}_{0+}=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~,~\bar{V}_{0-}=\left(1-k\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}~,~\bar{m}=m\left(1-k^{2}\right).

•

\bar{U}_{7}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}r^{2}e^{-2\frac{\left(k^{2}-2\right)}{k}\theta}+mr^{2}e^{2k\theta}=\bar{V}_{0}\frac{1}{\bar{y}^{2}}\left(\frac{\bar{x}}{\bar{y}}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}+\bar{m}\bar{x}\bar{y}

(9.133)

where
\bar{V}_{0}=V_{0}\frac{\left(1-k^{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{k}-1}}{\left(1-k\right)^{\frac{4}{k}}}
.

•

and the free particle potential.

\bar{U}_{8}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=0

Working as in the case

\displaystyle\bar{x}\left(t\right)

\bar{U}_{2}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=V_{0}\left(k+1\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}~\bar{x}^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}

\bar{U}_{5}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=\bar{V}_{0+}~\bar{x}^{\frac{{}^{2}}{1+k}}+\bar{m}\bar{x}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{1}\sinh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\bar{y}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)\left(\bar{y}_{1}-\frac{2\omega}{\bar{m}}\int\frac{E-\bar{x}_{1}\bar{V}_{0+}\sinh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)^{\frac{2}{1+k}}}{\bar{x}_{1}\left(\cosh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)+1\right)}\right)

where

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\frac{2\omega}{\bar{m}}\int\frac{E-\bar{y}_{1}\bar{V}_{0-}\sinh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)^{\frac{2}{1-k}}}{\bar{y}_{1}\left(\cosh\left(\omega t+\omega_{0}\right)+1\right)}\right)

.
\left(\bar{U}_{4}\text{ is for }\bar{m}=0\right)
.

This is again the Ermakov Piney potential. To solve it we go to spherical
coordinates

\bar{x}=ze^{w}~\ ,~\bar{y}=ze^{-w}~

(9.141)

in which the Lagrangian takes the form

L\left(z,w,\dot{z},\dot{w}\right)=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(-\dot{z}^{2}+z^{2}\dot{w}^{2}\right)-\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{z^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}-\bar{m}z^{2}

(9.142)

and the Hamiltonian

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(-\dot{z}^{2}+z^{2}\dot{w}^{2}\right)+\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{z^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}+\bar{m}z^{2}.

(9.143)

The Ermakov-Lewis invariant is

J_{EL}=z^{4}\dot{w}^{2}+2\frac{\bar{V}_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}e^{\frac{4}{k}w}

(9.144)

which when replaced in the Hamiltonian gives

E=-\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\dot{z}^{2}+N_{0}^{2}\frac{J_{EL}}{2z^{2}}+\bar{m}z^{2}.

(9.145)

This is the Hamiltonian of the Ermakov Pinney equation:

\ddot{z}-2\bar{m}z-N_{0}^{2}\frac{J_{EL}}{z^{3}}=0

(9.146)

whose solution is

\displaystyle z\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(l_{0}z_{1}\left(t\right)+l_{1}z_{2}\left(t\right)+l_{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(9.147)

\displaystyle e^{\frac{4}{k}w\left(t\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N_{0}^{2}J_{EL}}{2\bar{V}_{0}}\left[1-\tanh^{2}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{J_{EL}}}{k}\left(\int\frac{dt}{z^{2}\left(t\right)}+l_{4}\right)\right)\right]

(9.148)

where
z_{1,2}\left(t\right)
are solutions of the ode
\ddot{z}-2\bar{m}z=0
and
l_{0-4}
are constants.

\bar{U}_{8}\left(\bar{x},\bar{y}\right)=0

This is the free particle whose solution is

(9.149)

\displaystyle E

9.5.4
Case B: The 2d metric is conformally flat

In this case it is preferable to work with (
9.40
) which under the
coordinate transformation (
9.41
) becomes:

L=N^{2}\left(\theta\right)\left[-\frac{1}{2}\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}\right]-r^{2}V\left(\theta\right).

(9.151)

The kinetic metric in this case is not flat (i.e.
R_{\left(2\right)}\neq 0)
but of course it is conformally flat being a two dimensional metric. Its
conformal algebra is infinity dimensional however it has a closed subalgebra
consisting of the following vectors (this is the special conformal algebra
of
M^{2}
):

\displaystyle X^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh\theta\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{r}\sinh\theta\partial_{\theta}~~,~X^{2}=\sinh\theta\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{r}\cosh\theta\partial_{\theta}

\displaystyle X^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}~~\ ,~X^{4}=r\partial_{r}~~,~X^{5}=\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\cosh\theta\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{2}r\sinh\theta\partial_{\theta}

\displaystyle X^{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\sinh\theta\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{2}r\cosh\theta\partial_{\theta}

(9.152)

Writing
L_{X^{I}}g_{ij}=2C_{I}\left(r,\theta\right)g_{ij}
we find the
conformal factors of the CKVs
X^{I}

I=1,...6
above in terms of the the
conformal function. The result is:

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(r,\theta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r}\sinh\left(\theta\right)\frac{N_{,\theta}}{N}~,~C_{2}\left(r,\theta\right)=-\frac{1}{r}\cosh\left(\theta\right)\frac{N_{,\theta}}{N}

\displaystyle C_{3}\left(r,\theta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N_{,\theta}}{N}~~,~C_{4}\left(r,\theta\right)=1~,~~C_{5}\left(r,\theta\right)=\frac{r}{2}\left(\frac{2N\cosh\theta+\sinh\theta N_{\theta}}{N}\right)

\displaystyle C_{6}\left(r,\theta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}\left(\frac{2N\sinh\theta+\cosh\theta N_{\theta}}{N}\right)

(9.153)

The
N\left(\theta\right)\neq e^{c\theta}
, otherwise the kinetic metric
of the Lagrangian (
9.151
) is flat (the Ricci scalar vanishes) and
we return to the previous case A. This means that the vectors
X^{I}

I=1,...6
except the
I=4
are proper CKVs therefore they do not give (in
general) a Noether symmetry. The vector
X_{4}
is a non-gradient HV which
does not also produce a Noether symmetry. Therefore according to theorem
4.3.2
only Killing vectors are
possible to serve as Noether symmetries. KVs do not exist but for special
forms of the conformal function
N(\theta)
. Each such form of
N(\theta)

results in a potential
V(\theta)\
hence to a scalar field potential which
admits Noether point symmetries. In the following we determine the possible
N(\theta)
which lead to a KV and give the corresponding Noether symmetry
and the corresponding Noether integral which will be used for the solution
of the field equations.

1.

If
N\left(\theta\right)=\frac{N_{0}}{\cosh\left(2\theta\right)-1}
then
X^{5}
is a non gradient KV and a Noether symmetry for the
Lagrangian (
9.151
) for the potential

V\left(\theta\right)=\frac{V_{0}}{\cosh\left(2\theta\right)-1}~\text{or }V\left(\theta\right)=0

(9.154)

The corresponding Noether integral is

I_{X^{5}}=\frac{N_{0}^{2}r^{2}}{\left(\cosh\left(2\theta\right)-1\right)^{2}}\left(r\dot{\theta}\sinh\theta-\dot{r}\cosh\theta\right).

(9.155)

2.

If
N\left(\theta\right)=\frac{N_{0}}{\cosh\left(2\theta\right)+1}
then
X^{6}
is a non gradient KV,
X^{6}
and a Noether symmetry for
the Lagrangian (
9.151
) if

V\left(\theta\right)=\frac{V_{0}}{\cosh\left(2\theta\right)+1}~\text{or }V\left(\theta\right)=0

(9.156)

The corresponding Noether integral is

I_{X^{6}}=\frac{N_{0}^{2}r^{2}}{\left(\cosh\left(2\theta\right)+1\right)^{2}}\left(r\dot{\theta}\cosh\theta-\dot{r}\sinh\theta\right)

(9.157)

3.

If
N\left(\theta\right)=\frac{N_{0}}{\cosh^{2}\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)}
then the linear combination
X^{56}=c_{1}X^{5}+c_{2}X^{6}
where
c_{1}=\sinh\left(\theta_{0}\right)

and
c_{2}=\cosh\left(\theta_{0}\right)
.
X^{56}
is a Noether symmetry
for the Lagrangian (
9.151
) if

V\left(\theta\right)=\frac{V_{0}}{\cosh^{2}\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)}~\text{or }V\left(\theta\right)=0

(9.158)

with Noether integral

I_{X^{56}}=\frac{N_{0}^{2}r^{2}}{\cosh^{4}\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)}\left(r\dot{\theta}\cosh\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)-\dot{r}\sinh\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)\right)

Obviously case 3 is the most general and contains cases 1 and 2 (and the
trivial case) as special cases. Therefore in the following we look for
analytic solutions for the vector
X^{56}
only.

We recall that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-2F\left(\theta\right)}}=N^{2}\left(\theta\right)
from which follows:

~F\left(\theta\right)=-\frac{1}{2N_{0}^{4}}\cosh^{8}\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)~,N_{0}\in\mathbb{R}.

(9.159)

We may consider
\theta_{0}=0
(e.g. by introducing the new variable
\Theta=\theta+\theta_{0}).

For the potential (
9.158
) the Lagrangian (
9.151
) becomes

L=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{\cosh^{4}\theta}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}\right]-r^{2}\frac{V_{0}}{\cosh^{2}\theta}

(9.160)

and the Hamiltonian

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{\cosh^{4}\theta}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}\right]+r^{2}\frac{V_{0}}{\cosh^{2}\theta}.

(9.161)

The equations of motion are:

\displaystyle\ddot{r}+r\dot{\theta}^{2}-4\tanh\theta~\dot{r}\dot{\theta}-2\frac{V_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}r\cosh^{2}\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(9.162)

\displaystyle\ddot{\theta}-2\tanh\theta~\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\dot{r}^{2}+\dot{\theta}^{2}\right)+\frac{2}{r}\dot{r}\dot{\theta}-2\frac{V_{0}}{N_{0}^{2}}\cosh\theta\sinh\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(9.163)

and the Noether integral
I_{X^{56}}
for
\theta_{0}=0
becomes:

I_{X^{56}}=\frac{N_{0}^{2}r^{2}}{\cosh^{4}\left(\theta+\theta_{0}\right)}\left(r\dot{\theta}\cosh\left(\theta\right)-\dot{r}\sinh\left(\theta\right)\right).

(9.164)

In order to proceed with the solution of the system of equations (
9.162
), (
9.163
) we change to the coordinates
x,y~
which we
define by the relations

r=\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}y^{2}}}~,~\theta=\arctan h\left(xy\right).

(9.165)

In the coordinates
x,y
the Lagrangian and the Hamiltonian become:

L=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(-\dot{x}^{2}+x^{4}\dot{y}\right)-V_{0}x^{2}

(9.166)

E=\frac{N_{0}^{2}}{2}\left(-\dot{x}^{2}+x^{4}\dot{y}^{2}\right)+V_{0}x^{2}

(9.167)

and the Noether integral (we write
I
for
I_{X^{56}})

I=x^{4}\dot{y}.

(9.168)

Let us assume that
I\neq 0.
In the new variables the Euler - Lagrange
equations read:

\displaystyle\ddot{x}+2x^{3}\dot{y}^{2}-2V_{0}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(9.169)

\displaystyle\ddot{y}+\frac{4}{x}\dot{x}\dot{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(9.170)

From the Noether integral we have
\dot{y}=\frac{I}{x^{4}}~
which
upon substitution in the field equations gives the equation

\displaystyle\ddot{x}-2V_{0}x+\frac{2I^{2}}{x^{5}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(9.171)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(-\dot{x}^{2}+\frac{I^{2}}{x^{4}}\right)+V_{0}x^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E.

(9.172)

from which we compute

\dot{x}=\sqrt{\frac{I^{2}}{x^{4}}+2V_{0}x^{2}-2E}

(9.173)

and finally the analytic solution

\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{\frac{I^{2}}{x^{4}}+2V_{0}x^{2}-2E}}=t-t_{0}.

(9.174)

From the Noether integral we find

y\left(t\right)-y_{0}=\int\frac{I}{x^{4}}dt.

(9.175)

If
I=0
then the analytic solution is

x=x_{0}\sinh\left(\sqrt{2V_{0}}t+x_{1}\right),~y=y_{0}

(9.176)

with Hamiltonian constrain
E=-x_{0}^{2}V_{0}.

If
V_{0}=0~
(i.e. free particle) and
I=0
the analytic solution
is

x=x_{0}t+x_{1}~,~y=y_{0}

(9.177)

with Hamiltonian constrain
E=-\frac{1}{2}x_{0}^{2}.

9.6
Noether point symmetries of a minimally coupled Scalar field.

In this section we study the Noether point symmetries of a minimally coupled
scalar field in a spatially flat FRW spacetime. The action of the field
equations is

S_{M}=\int d\tau dx^{3}\sqrt{-g}\left[R+\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}\phi^{;i}\phi^{;j}-V\left(\phi\right)\right]+\int L_{m}d\tau dx^{3}.

(9.178)

where
L_{m}
is the Lagrangian of the dust matter fluid. For a spatially
flat FRW spacetime the Ricciscalar is

R=6\left(\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}+\frac{\dot{a}^{2}}{a^{2}}\right)

hence, the Lagrangian of the field equation is (
9.12
) and the field
equations are

E=-3a\dot{a}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}a^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}+a^{3}V\left(\phi\right)

(9.179)

\ddot{a}+\frac{1}{2a}\dot{a}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\dot{\phi}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}aV=0

(9.180)

\ddot{\phi}+\frac{3}{a}\dot{a}\dot{\phi}+\varepsilon V_{,\phi}=0

(9.181)

From the kinetic term of (
9.12
) we define the two dimensional metric

ds^{2}=-6a~da^{2}+\varepsilon a^{3}d\phi^{2}.

(9.182)

We find that the curvature of the
\{a,\phi\}
space is
R=0
implying
flatness (since all
2
dimensional spaces are Einstein spaces hence
\hat{R}=0
implies that the space is flat). Also, the signature of the metric eq.(
9.182
) is
0
, hence the space is the 2-d Minkowski space. In order
to simplify the field equations we apply the following coordinate
transformation

r=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}a^{3/2}\;\;\;\;\;\theta=\sqrt{\frac{3k\epsilon}{8}}\phi\;,

(9.183)

in the new coordinates the two dimensional metric (
9.182
) is given by

d{\hat{s}}^{2}=-dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2}

(9.184)

that is,
(r,\theta)
are hyperbolic spherical coordinates in the two
dimensional Minkowski space
\{a,\phi\}
. Next we introduce the new
coordinates
(x,y)
with the transformation:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=r\cosh\;(\theta)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=r\sinh\;(\theta)

(9.185)

which implies that the metric (
9.184
) becomes
d\hat{s}^{2}=-dx^{2}+dy^{2}
. We also point here that

r^{2}=x^{2}-y^{2}\;\;\;\;\;\theta=\mathrm{arctanh}(y/x)\;.

(9.186)

The scale factor (
a(t)>0
) is now given by:

a=\left[\frac{3(x^{2}-y^{2})}{8}\right]^{1/3}

(9.187)

which means that the new variables have to satisfy the following inequality:

x\geq|y|
.

In the new coordinate system
(x,y)
the Lagrangian (
9.12
) and the
Hamiltonian (
9.179
) are written:

L=\frac{1}{2}\left(\dot{y}^{2}-\dot{x}^{2}\right)-V_{eff}(x,y)

(9.188)

E=\frac{1}{2}\left(\dot{y}^{2}-\dot{x}^{2}\right)+V_{eff}(x,y)

(9.189)

where

V_{eff}(x,y)=\left(x^{2}-y^{2}\right)\tilde{V}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right).

(9.190)

Note that we have used

\tilde{V}\left(\theta\right)=\frac{3k}{8}V(\theta)\;.

(9.191)

We now proceed in an attempt to provide the Noether point symmetries of the
current dynamical problem using the results of chapter
4
.

Since the Lagrangian (
9.188
) is autonomous admits the Noether point
symmetry
\partial_{t}
with Noether integral the Hamiltonian (
9.189
). Lagrangian (
9.188
) admits extra Noether point symmetries in the
following cases.

9.6.1
Hyperbolic - UDM Potential

Hyperbolic - UDM Potential:
Generically, we use the following
potential:

\tilde{V}(\theta)=\frac{\omega_{1}\cosh^{2}\left(\theta\right)-\omega_{2}\sinh^{2}\left(\theta\right)}{2}

(9.192)

or

V_{eff}(x,y)=r^{2}\tilde{V}(\theta)=\frac{\omega_{1}x^{2}-\omega_{2}y^{2}}{2}

(9.193)

The corresponding Noether symmetries,
X_{n}
, are known (see for example

[
5
]
). These are:

\displaystyle X_{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}~,~X_{n_{2}}=\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)\partial_{x}~,~X_{n_{3}}=\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)\partial_{x}

\displaystyle X_{n_{4}}

\displaystyle=\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)\partial_{y}~,~X_{n_{5}}=\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)\partial_{y}

The Noether integrals are the Hamiltonian and the quantities:

\displaystyle I_{n_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)\dot{x}-\sqrt{\omega_{1}}\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)x

\displaystyle I_{n_{3}}

\displaystyle=\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)\dot{x}-\sqrt{\omega_{1}}\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{1}}t\right)x

\displaystyle I_{n_{4}}

\displaystyle=\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)\dot{y}-\sqrt{\omega_{2}}\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)y

\displaystyle I_{n_{5}}

\displaystyle=\cosh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)\dot{y}-\sqrt{\omega_{2}}\sinh\left(\sqrt{\omega_{2}}t\right)y

Obviously the UDM potential is a particular case of the current general
hyperbolic potential. Indeed for
\omega_{1}=2\omega_{2}
and with the aid
of eqs.(
9.183
), (
9.191
) we fully recover the UDM potential

[
153
,
154
,
155
,
142
]

V(\phi)=V_{0}\left[1+\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{3k\epsilon}{8}\phi\right)\right]

(9.194)

where
V_{0}=\frac{4\omega_{2}}{3k}
modulus a constant.

9.6.2
Exponential Potential

Exponential Potential:
The exponential potential

V_{eff}(r,\theta)=r^{2}\tilde{V}(\theta)=r^{2}e^{-d\theta}\;.

admits the extra Noether symmetry

X_{n}=2t\partial_{t}+\left(x+\frac{4}{d}y\right)\partial_{x}+\left(y+\frac{4}{d}x\right)\partial_{y}\;.

(9.195)

In general the Noether integral for the vector
X_{n}=2t\partial_{t}+\eta^{i}\partial_{i}
is

I=2tE+\left(x+\frac{4}{d}y\right)\dot{x}-\left(y+\frac{4}{d}x\right)\dot{y}

(9.196)

where
E
is the Hamiltonian. Using
{\tilde{V}}=e^{-d\theta}
together
with eq.(
9.183
) and eq.(
9.191
) we write the potential to its
nominal form
[
156
]
which is

V(\phi)=V_{0}\mathrm{exp}\left(-d\;\sqrt{\frac{3k\epsilon}{8}}\phi\right)

(9.197)

where
V_{0}=\frac{8}{3k}
modulus a constant
3
3

3

In the special case of
d=2
, the system admits an additional Lie symmetry
\partial_{x}+\partial_{y}
, with Noether integral
I=\dot{x}-\dot{y}.
.

We note that the analytic solutions of sections
9.5.3
and
9.5.4
are also solutions for the minimally coupled Lagrangian (
9.12
) if and only if the space does not admit dust.

9.7
The Lie and Noether symmetries of Bianchi class A homogeneous
cosmologies with a scalar field.

The class of Bianchi spatially homogeneous cosmologies contains many
important cosmological models, including the standard FRW model. In these
models the spacetime manifold is foliated along the time axis, with three
dimensional homogeneous hypersurfaces. Bianchi has classified all three
dimensional real Lie algebras and has shown that there are nine of them.
This results in nine types (two of them being families of spacetimes) of
Bianchi spatially homogeneous spacetimes. The principal advantage of Bianchi
cosmological models is that, in these models the physical variables depend
on the time only, reducing the Einstein and other governing equations to
ordinary differential equations.

The Bianchi models are studied in the well known ADM decomposition (
[
157
,
158
]
) according to which the metric is written

ds^{2}=-N^{2}(t)dt^{2}+g_{\mu\nu}\omega^{\mu}\otimes\omega^{\nu}

(9.198)

where
N(t)
is the lapse function and
\{\omega^{a}\}
is the canonical
basis of 1-forms which satisfy the Lie algebra

d\omega^{i}=C_{jk}^{i}\omega^{j}\wedge\omega^{k}.

(9.199)

C_{jk}^{i}
are the structure constants of the algebra. The spatial metric
g_{\mu\nu}
splits so that

g_{\mu\nu}=\exp(2\lambda)\exp(-2\beta)_{\mu\nu}

(9.200)

where
\exp(2\lambda)
is the scale factor of the universe and
\beta_{\mu\nu}
is a
3\times 3
symmetric, traceless matrix, which can be
written in a diagonal form with two independent quantities, the so called
anisotropy parameters
\beta_{+},\beta_{-}
, as follows:

\beta_{\mu\nu}=diag\left(\beta_{+},-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{+}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\beta_{-},-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{+}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\beta_{-}\right).

(9.201)

The Bianchi models are grouped in classes A and B by means of a vector
a^{\mu}
and a symmetric 3
\times 3
metric
n_{\mu\nu}
which are
constrainted by the condition
n_{\mu\nu}a^{\nu}=0.
Class A is defined
by
a^{\mu}=0
and Class B by
a^{\mu}\neq 0.
Each Class is divided into
several types according to the rank and (the modulus of the) signature of
n^{\mu\nu}.
Because of the difficulty in formulating the class B Bianchi
models in the ADM formalism, it is usually the case that one confines
attention to the class A models. Furthermore it is well known that for the
class A models there is a Lagrangian
[
159
]
whereas for the
class B models, to the author’s knowledge, no such Lagrangian seems to
exist. Details on the structure and the Physics of the Bianchi models can be
found e.g. in
[
157
,
159
]
.

Research in Physics on inflationary models has shown the importance of
scalar fields in cosmology
[
160
]
. This has raised interest in
the dynamics of Bianchi spacetimes filled with a scalar field, with an
arbitrary self interaction potential, minimally coupled to the gravitational
field
[
161
]
. The Lagrangian leading to the full Bianchi scalar field
dynamics is

L=e^{3\lambda}\left[R^{\ast}+6\lambda-\frac{3}{2}(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2})-\dot{\phi}^{2}+V(\phi)\right]

(9.202)

where
R^{\ast}
is the Ricci scalar of the
3
dimensional spatial
hypersurfaces given by the expression:

\displaystyle R^{\ast}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\lambda}\left[N_{1}^{2}e^{4\beta_{1}}+e^{-2\beta_{1}}\left(N_{2}e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}-N_{3}e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)^{2}-2N_{1}e^{\beta_{1}}\left(N_{2}e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}-N_{3}e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}(1+N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}).

The constants
N_{1},N_{2},
and
N_{3}
are the components of the
classification vector
n^{\mu}
and
\beta_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{+}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\beta_{-},

\beta_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{+}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\beta_{-}
. It is important to note that the curvature scalar
R^{\ast}
does not depend on the derivatives of the anisotropy parameters
\beta_{+},

\beta_{-}
, equivalently on
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}.

The Euler Lagrange equations due to the Lagrangian (
9.202
) are
[
86
]
:

\displaystyle\ddot{\lambda}+\frac{3}{2}\dot{\lambda}^{2}+\frac{3}{8}(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2})+\frac{1}{4}\dot{\phi}^{2}-\frac{1}{12}e^{-3\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left(e^{3\lambda}R^{\ast}\right)-\frac{1}{2}V(\phi)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\ddot{\beta}_{1}+3\dot{\lambda}\dot{\beta}_{1}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{\partial R^{\ast}}{\partial\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\ddot{\beta}_{2}+3\dot{\lambda}\dot{\beta}_{2}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{\partial R^{\ast}}{\partial\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3\dot{\phi}\dot{\lambda}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=0

where a dot over a symbol indicates derivative with respect to
t.

In the following we apply Theorem
4.2.2
and
Theorem
4.3.2
and compute
the Lie and the Noether point symmetries of class A Bianchi models. The Lie
and the Noether point symmetries of Bianchi class A models with a scalar
field have also been computed in
[
86
,
143
,
139
]
. However, as
it will be shown, these studies are not complete, in the sense that they
have not found all Noether symmetries. Furthermore our approach is entirely
different than the classical Lie approach employed in these works. Finally
it is general and can be applied without difficulty to Class B spacetimes.

We consider the four dimensional Riemannian space with coordinates
x^{i}=\left(\lambda,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\phi\right)
and metric

ds^{2}=e^{3\lambda}\left(12d\lambda^{2}-3d\beta_{1}^{2}-3d\beta_{2}^{2}-2d\phi^{2}\right).

(9.203)

The metric (
9.203
) is the conformally flat FRW spacetime whose
special projective algebra consists of the non gradient KVs
[
46
,
45
]

\displaystyle Y^{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta_{1}},~Y^{2}=\partial_{\beta_{2}},~Y^{3}=\partial_{\phi},~Y^{4}=\beta_{2}\partial_{\beta_{1}}-\beta_{1}\partial_{\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle~Y^{5}

\displaystyle=\phi\partial_{\beta_{1}}-\frac{3}{2}\beta_{1}\partial_{\phi},~Y^{6}=\phi\partial_{\beta_{2}}-\frac{3}{2}\beta_{2}\partial_{\phi}

and the gradient HV

H^{i}=\frac{2}{3}\partial_{\lambda}~,~\psi=1.

The Lagrangian (
9.202
) is written:

L=T-U(x^{i})

where
T=\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}\dot{x}^{i}\dot{x}^{i}
is the geodesic
Lagrangian,
U(x^{i})
is the potential function

U(x^{i})=-e^{3\lambda}\left(V\left(\phi\right)+R^{\ast}\right)

(9.204)

and we have used the fact that the curvature scalar does not depend on the
derivatives of the coordinates
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}.
Now we apply Theorem
4.2.2
and Theorem
4.3.2
to determine the Lie and the point
Noether symmetries of the dynamical system with Lagrangian (
9.202
).

In order to compute the potential
U(x^{i})
we need the expression of
R^{\ast}
for each Bianchi type. We find for the Class A models

Bianchi I:
R^{\ast}=0

Bianchi II:
R^{\ast}=-e^{\left(2\beta_{1}-\lambda\right)}

Bianchi VI
0
: Class A. ,
R^{\ast}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\lambda}\left(e^{4\beta_{1}}+e^{-2\left(\beta_{1}-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}\right)}+2e^{\beta_{1}+\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)

Bianchi VII
0
: Class A.,
R^{\ast}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\lambda}\left(e^{4\beta_{1}}+e^{-2\left(\beta_{1}-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}\right)}-2e^{\beta_{1}+\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)

Bianchi VIII:
R^{\ast}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\lambda}\left(e^{4\beta_{1}}+e^{-2\beta_{1}}\left(e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}+e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)^{2}-2e^{\beta_{1}}\left(e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}-e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}x\right)\right)

Bianchi IX:
R^{\ast}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\lambda}\left(e^{4\beta_{1}}+e^{-2\beta_{1}}\left(e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}+e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}\right)^{2}-2e^{\beta_{1}}\left(e^{\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}-e^{-\sqrt{3}\beta_{2}}x\right)\right)+1
.

We determine the Lie and the Noether point symmetries in the following cases:

Case 1. Vacuum. In this case
\phi=
constant and the metric (
9.203
)
reduces to the three dimensional FRW metric.

Case 2. Zero potential
V\left(\phi\right)=0,

\dot{\phi}\neq 0

Case 3. Constant Potential
V\left(\phi\right)=
constant,
\dot{\phi}\neq 0

Case 4. Arbitrary Potential
V\left(\phi\right),~\dot{\phi}\neq 0.

9.7.1
Bianchi I

Case 1.

In this case
\dot{\phi}=0
,
V\left(\phi\right)=0
and the Lagrangian
becomes
~L=e^{3\lambda}\left[6\dot{\lambda}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2}\right)\right]~
hence the potential
U(x^{\mu})=0
where
x^{\mu}=(\lambda,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}).
The
auxiliary metric is
ds^{2}=e^{3\lambda}\left(12d\lambda^{2}-3d\beta_{1}^{2}-3d\beta_{2}^{2}\right).
The special PCs of this metric are the
non gradient KVs
Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{4}~
and the gradient HV
H^{i}
.

From Theorem
4.2.2
we find that the Lie
point symmetries are the vectors

\partial_{t},~t\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{4},H^{i},~t^{2}\partial_{t}+tH^{i}

which coincide with those found in
[
86
]
. From Theorem
4.3.2
we find that the Noether point
symmetries are

\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{4},~2t\partial_{t}+H^{i},t^{2}\partial_{t}+tH^{i}

i.e. we find two more Noether symmetries than
[
86
]
.

Case 2
.
V\left(\phi\right)=0\,,

\dot{\phi}\neq 0

In this case the Lagrangian is
~L=e^{3\lambda}\left[6\dot{\lambda}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2}\right)-\dot{\phi}^{2}\right]~
and the potential function
U(x^{i})=0.
The auxiliary metric
is (
9.203
). From Theorem
4.2.2
we
find that the Lie point symmetries are

\partial_{t},~~t\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{3},~Y^{4},~Y^{5},~Y^{6},~H^{i},~t^{2}\partial_{t}+tH^{i}~

and coincide with those found in
[
86
]
. Application of Theorem
4.3.2
gives that the Noether
point symmetries are

\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{3},~Y^{4},~Y^{5},~Y^{6},~2t\partial_{t}+H^{i},t^{2}\partial_{t}+tH^{i}~

i.e. two more from the ones found in
[
86
]
.

Case 3.

V\left(\phi\right)=C_{0},~\dot{\phi}\neq 0

The Lagrangian is
~L=e^{3\lambda}\left[6\dot{\lambda}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2}\right)-\dot{\phi}^{2}+C_{0}\right]~
hence the potential
U(x^{i})=-C_{0}e^{3\lambda}.~
Using Theorem
4.2.2
we find that the Lie point symmetries are

\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{3},~Y^{4},~Y^{5},~Y^{6},~H^{i},~\frac{1}{C}e^{\pm Ct}\partial_{t}\pm e^{\pm Ct}H^{i}

where
C=\frac{\sqrt{6C_{0}}}{2}
, and coincide with those found in
[
86
]
. Application of Theorem
4.3.2
gives the Noether point symmetries

\partial_{t},~Y^{1},~Y^{2},~Y^{3},~Y^{4},~Y^{5},~Y^{6},~\frac{1}{C}e^{\pm Ct}\partial_{t}\pm e^{\pm Ct}H^{i}.~

Again we find two more Noether symmetries than
[
86
]
.

Case 4.

V\left(\phi\right)=
arbitrary
\dot{\phi}\neq 0

In this case the Lagrangian is
L=e^{3\lambda}\left[6\dot{\lambda}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\dot{\beta}_{1}^{2}+\dot{\beta}_{2}^{2}\right)-\dot{\phi}^{2}+V\left(\phi\right)\right]~
and the potential
U(x^{i})=-e^{3\lambda}V\left(\phi\right)
. Application of Theorem
4.2.2
gives the Lie point symmetries
~\partial_{t}~,~Y^{1}~,~Y^{2}~,~Y^{4}~~,~H^{i}~\
and application of Theorem
4.3.2
gives the Noether symmetries
\partial_{t}~,~Y^{1}~,~Y^{2}~,~Y^{4}.
